| Worst matchup | Venkat vs Strand 70% [65%] | Strand vs Begay 33% | 40–60% (noise ±12 at 71 games) |
| Priority A win rate | 50.3% | 51.7% | 47–53% ✓ |
| Comeback rate | 36.6% | 38.3% | 20–40% ✓ |
| Median margin / 40+ EV / 100+ EV | 58 / **65.0%** / **25.6%** [24.7%] | 54 / 62.4% / 20.1% | ≤ 60 / ≤ 65% / ≤ 25% |
| Money raised per player / weeks 1–3 ÷ 10–12 | $38.1 / 0.57 | $50.1 / 1.02 (measured on this branch) | within 25% / ≤ 1.5 ✓ |
| GOTV changes the winner (spend) | 10.1% ($8.3) | 11.8% ($7.6) | ≥ 10% ✓ |
| Debates change the winner (EV swung) | 17.7% (19.3) [19.2% (20.1)] | 16.4% (15.3) | 15–30% (20–40) ✓ |
| Debate topic win rates | 38–40%, Hale 48% | 41–44%, Hale 53% | Hale ≤ 60% ✓ |
| 2-point prep edge wins / loses a topic | 81% / 6% | 69% / 20% | ≥ 75% / ≤ 12% ✓ |
| Underdog wrong-leader weeks / tier changes | 18.9% / 3.19 | 21.8% / 3.10 | < 10% / ≤ 3 (D-020: structural) |
| Random / fundraise / rally / attack / never-poll / sandbag | 0 / 15.5 / 42.5 / 41 / 51 [55.2] / 39% | 0 / 0.8 / 43 / 40 / 54.5 / 40% | ≤ 5, < 55, ≤ 50 |

Red after this pass: Venkat and the 40+/100+ EV shares sit on their lines (noise-level at 2,000, inside at 6,000 for 100+ EV), Strand and Alvarado are the information/start-cash blind spots (D-037), the two rubber-band flags are D-020's, and NeverPoll is the pass-1 poll-value question. Everything that passed in D-027 still passes except the one matchup cell, which moves with the seed set.

*Proposals for the humans (not done here; each changes a mechanic's intent or a part another branch owns):*
1. **The tied block** (D-019, D-024) is what caps the debate and Choice payoffs: about 13 undrafted, unleaned states finish at exactly 35/35 in bot games and any national shift decides all of them at once. Humans rally those states (the playtesters did), bots do not. A bot that values tipping-point states, or leans on more states, would let debates be worth more than 0.3 a topic.
2. **A bot that reads Strand's preview** beyond Regional weeks (hold cash for a Windfall, hold ads for a Spotlight issue), and values polls at all, so the harness can measure information perks. Until then Strand and Alvarado will read 44–47%.
3. **Week-12 fundraisers** still pay money nobody can spend. The screen should warn (client branch); a rule change (e.g. week-12 fundraisers add to the popular vote tiebreak) was not approved.
4. **Calloway's 4th AP vs Okafor** and **nominations stripping big strongholds**: the playtesters flagged both; the harness has Calloway at 53% and the lean/draft checks green, so nothing was changed. If the humans want nominations softer, "a nominated stronghold keeps half its bonus" is the smallest rule change to test.
5. **Venkat** at the 55% line: "media $2.5" or "income $2 and repeat buys keep 60%" are the next notches if human play confirms her edge.

## Issue-based choices and debates (Oct 22, 2026, branch `issue-choices`)

Approved by the designers after playtest 2: Choice events and debates must be skill-based, tied to each state's issues, and visibly matter. Their words: debates deciding about one match in five is fine, but "in those 20% of games it isn't obvious that the debates did anything unless you do a nerd breakdown of the stats"; the D-036 Choice matrix "has caused confusion and made choice events feel too random", and the direction is "a right answer would be helpful, but it may decrease the politician's popularity in states where that position is unpopular, so you have to answer based on the issues of the states you need to win." Both mechanics' intent changed, which they approved. Every spec below failed before the code.

### D-039 — Choice events are stances on an issue (approved reshape)

**Rules.** The deck has 24 cards: the eight Spotlights, five Regionals and three Windfalls of D-027 plus **eight Choice events, one per issue** (`Sim/Events.luau`: `plant` Jobs, `walkout` Health, `calendar` Schools, `river` Environment, `patrol` Safety, `toll` Transit, `datacenter` Technology, `water` Farms). A match deals 10, so it sees about 3.3 Choice weeks (0.4% of matches see none). Each Choice event is a fictional dispute over its issue and offers three stance slots, always in this shape: **A** and **B** are opposed sides, **C** is the hedge. A stance is data: a name, a line of flavor, and the issues it `likes` and `dislikes` (B likes what A dislikes and vice versa; C likes nothing).

A state with the event's issue among its two top issues reacts by its *other* top issue: if the stance likes that issue, +`STANCE_POINTS` (1.5) support for the candidate who took it; if it dislikes it, −1.5; otherwise +`STANCE_HEDGE` (0.5). Every other state hears a side a little through its own top issues: +`STANCE_SPILL` (0.5) per issue the stance likes, −0.5 per issue it dislikes (so a state whose two issues are one liked and one disliked does not move). The hedge gives +0.5 in every state with the issue and nothing elsewhere. A candidate whose platform carries the event's issue is heard harder in the issue's states: ×`STANCE_PLATFORM_MULT` (1.5), clamped to ±`EVENT_CAP` (2). Gains use the normal undecided/opponent split, losses go to undecided, and both players' stances are applied together from one start-of-step snapshot (D-001), after pivots, where the D-036 shift used to be. A missing stance, an empty plan or a timeout is the hedge (`Events.defaultChoice()`); any value outside `"A" | "B" | "C"` is `BadChoice`, a stance outside a Choice week `UnexpectedChoice`, exactly as before.

**Why this shape.** The reaction is a pure function of fixed state data (`StateData` issues) and the public platform, so the same event moves the same states the same way in every match and a player can learn them; the client previews every stance on the map with the same function the server applies (`Events.stanceEffects(config, event, id, platform)`, `Events.stanceEffect` per state). The "right" answer is the stance whose moves are worth the most in the states *you* need, so it differs per player and per map, and there is nothing to guess about the opponent (the matrix is gone). Camps were drawn so each side's liked states hold at least a quarter of the issue's EV (spec), e.g. Jobs: A (Transit, Farms camp: GA, IN, MI, NV, SC, DE, IA, ND, 69 EV) against B (Health, Safety camp: AL, KY, PA, IL, TN, 66 EV), with OH, LA indifferent. The issue's states (8–15 of them) carry the big moves; the spillover (D-041) makes the rest of the map lean a little toward one side or the other, so a stance is about 25 points of gross movement that nets near zero, not a national shift that lands on the tied block (D-019). The hedge is the sure, small answer: with every camp state close it is right, and the bot takes it about one week in ten. Platform credibility is the one asymmetry between two candidates who say the same thing, and it gives platform choice a job beyond debate topics (playtest 2: "platform choice feels solved").

**What the clients get.** `View.event` for a Choice week carries `issue` and `stances = { { id, name, text } × 3 }`. After the week, `View.eventLog[k]` carries `choices` (each player's stance), `moves` (`{ state, points = { per player } }` for every state that reacted) and `flips` (leader changes projected on the start-of-week public polls: `Polls.projectFlips`, public arithmetic), and the week card's `choice` is the stance. The resolve event stream has one `Choice` event per player (`issue` = the stance id, `amount` = its points summed over states). Hidden: nothing new leaves the server; `StateSim.stanceShift` (the support each stance actually moved, for the harness recount) is forbidden in `View.spec` like `debateShift`.

**Rejected.** (1) Reactions by region instead of the other top issue: easier to see on a map, but the designers asked for issues, and a region split does not teach the state data. (2) Reactions that depend on the public poll (a stance wins over states you trail in, hurts where you lead): asymmetric and good for comebacks, but then the same event reacts differently every week and the leader always hedges. (3) Two stances only: the hedge is what makes "I need both camps" a real answer instead of a coin flip. (4) No spillover, `STANCE_POINTS` 2.0, `STANCE_HEDGE` 0 or 0.3, a stronger platform multiplier: none of them made the decision worth more than 52–54% against random stances (D-041); the spillover did.

### D-040 — Debates move states, not a national average (approved reshape)

**Rules.** The roll (0..3), prep, platform bonus and stat are unchanged, so a 2-point prep edge still wins its topic 81% of the time and loses 6%. What a topic is worth changed: winning it adds `DEBATE_TOPIC_WIN` (1.0) support in **every state where that topic is a top issue** (`Debate.topicStates`, fixed data) plus a flat `DEBATE_TOPIC_NATIONAL` (0.1) in every state; losing a topic you put 0 prep on costs `DEBATE_GAFFE` (0.6) in that topic's states; a 3–0 sweep adds a flat `DEBATE_SWEEP` (0.2) everywhere and the usual battleground momentum. Gains use the normal split, losses go to undecided, everything from one snapshot. Then the public polls are **republished with the same error terms** (`Polls.refresh`, no new draw), so the map and the projected EV show the debate the moment the week's plan opens.

**Why.** The old national shift was undecided-weighted (×undecided ÷ mean undecided), so its per-state effect leaked true undecided and could not be shown; 0.3 of it per topic was invisible on any single state. A topic win is now a visible +1.0 in 8–13 named states, "your Jobs win swung OH, MI and PA". `DEBATE_TOPIC_WIN` 1.2 measured debates deciding 25–26% of matches (target 15–25%), 1.0 measured 24%; the national part is deliberately a nudge (0.1 on a 35/35 tied-block state is nothing against election noise of 2), it exists so a debate is felt everywhere a little. The gaffe is worth more than before (0.6 in the topic's states against 0.3 national) because it is now a named, local cost. The poll republish is what makes the debate "obvious": before, the week-4 plan opened on polls that predated the debate.

**What the clients get.** `DebateResult` adds `gaffes` (`[player][topic]`), `moves` (`{ state, points = { per player } }`, the targeted points: topic wins and gaffes; the flat `national` per player applies everywhere on top) and `flips` (states whose public-poll leader changed when the polls were republished, with `from` and `to`, 0 = tied). The resolve events add one `PollFlip` per flipped state (`state`, `player` = new leader, `amount` = its EV, `issue = "Debate"`). `Debate.topicStates(topic)` and `Debate.odds(config, edge)` are public helpers for the prep screen. No-leak: every number in `moves` is public arithmetic on the result; the republished poll differs from the previous one by exactly those points (rounded), both of which are public.

### D-041 — Bot, skill check and the harness report (Oct 22, 2026)

**Bot.** `Bot.choose` takes the stance whose public per-state moves (`Events.stanceEffects`, with its own platform) are worth the most expected EV on its map: each state's points as margin (a gain also takes 1 − `GAIN_FROM_UNDECIDED` from the opponent; a loss goes to undecided) weighted by `EV × pdf(margin ÷ sigma) ÷ sigma`, the same closeness weight the rest of the bot uses. Ties go to the first slot, so no seat is favored. `Bot.prep` values each topic by what its states (plus the flat part everywhere) would be worth, then tries every legal split of its prep points (at most `DEBATE_MAX_PER_TOPIC` each) against `Debate.odds` of the edge over an opponent assumed to spread evenly, counting a zero's gaffe risk; the exhaustive search replaced a greedy one because the roll makes returns convex near a big deficit (the first point on a lost cause buys nothing, the fourth buys the topic). `Bot.spec` checks the stance follows the camp it needs (A, then B, then the hedge when both camps are close) and the prep follows the topic whose states are close. New style `RandomStance`: the standard bot with uniformly random stances.

**Skill check.** `tests/harness/skill.luau` plays the standard bot against `RandomStance` (seats alternating, two different candidates per match) and the balance harness repeats it at 800 games. The target is a RandomStance win rate ≤ 45% (the bot that reads the map wins ≥ 55%). The first numbers (`STANCE_POINTS` 1.5, `STANCE_HEDGE` 0.3, no spillover) measured **47.9% ± 1.5** over 4,000 matches: the decision was worth about 2 points of win rate. The reason is structural and worth knowing: both bots weigh the same close states, so they took the same stance in **91–93%** of Choice weeks, and two candidates who say the same thing move the same way (the recount with the stances removed changed the winner in only 3–4% of matches). Tried and rejected at 2,000 matches each: `STANCE_POINTS` 2.0 (46.0%), `STANCE_HEDGE` 0 (46.8%), a ×2 platform multiplier on 1.0 points (45.0%). What worked was the **spillover** (`STANCE_SPILL`): states that do not have the issue still move ±0.5 per top issue of theirs that a side likes or dislikes, so a stance is a map-wide tilt whose sign a player can read off the state data, not a nudge to a dozen states. 0.25 → 42.5%, 0.35 → 41.4%, 0.5 → 34.8%; with the hedge raised to 0.5 so it stays a real answer, 36.0%, and **39.0% ± 1.5 at 4,000 matches** once the platform multiplier was kept out of the spillover (an early build let it leak there). Final: `STANCE_SPILL` 0.5, `STANCE_HEDGE` 0.5: the bot that reads the map wins 61% of otherwise mirrored matches.

**Debates.** `DEBATE_TOPIC_WIN` 1.2 measured 24.9–26.4% of winners changed by the recount at 2,000 matches; 1.0 measured 23.8–25.4% and swings about 21 EV, so 1.0 is the number (the target band is 15–25% and 1.2 sat on its edge). Hale's topic win rate is unchanged at 47% (others 38–41%), so the prep perk is neither stronger nor weaker than in D-037.

**Harness report** (`lune run tests/harness/balance.luau`, 2,000 matches; "before" is the D-038 report measured on this branch's start).

| Check | After | Before (D-038) | Target |
|---|---|---|---|
| Candidates | **Okafor 57.7**, Calloway 50.3, **Venkat 56.7**, Hale 45.3, **Alvarado 44.8**, Reyes 49.8, **Strand 44.8**, Begay 51.1 | Okafor 54, Calloway 53, **Venkat 59**, Hale 49, **Alvarado 44**, Reyes 47, **Strand 44**, Begay 49 | 45–55% |
| Worst matchup | Alvarado vs Okafor 36% | Venkat vs Strand 70% | 40–60% (±12 at 71 games) |
| Priority A / comeback | 50.9% / 39.0% | 50.3% / 36.6% | 47–53% / 20–40% ✓ |
| Median margin / 40+ EV / 100+ EV | 52 / 61.3% / 20.8% | 58 / **65.0%** / **25.6%** | ≤ 60 / ≤ 65% / ≤ 25% ✓ |
| Money raised / weeks 1–3 ÷ 10–12 | $37.1 / 0.58 | $38.1 / 0.57 | ✓ |
| GOTV changes the winner (spend) | 12.2% ($8.4) | 10.1% ($8.3) | ≥ 10% ✓ |
| Debates change the winner (EV swung) | 23.2% (21.5) | 17.7% (19.3) | 15–25% (20–40) ✓ |
| Topic win rates | 38–41%, Hale 47% | 38–40%, Hale 48% | Hale ≤ 60% ✓ |
| Choice stances change the winner (EV swung) | 5.1% (4.7) | — (matrix: not measured) | informational |
| Choice events per match / stances A, B, C | 3.32 / 44, 36, 20% | 2 (4 of 20 cards) | — |
| Both players took the same stance | 90.7% | — | informational |
| RandomStance win rate | **39.1%** (39.0 ± 1.5 at 4,000) | — | ≤ 45% ✓ |
| Underdog wrong-leader weeks / tier changes | 17.4% / 3.12 | 18.9% / 3.19 | < 10% / ≤ 3 (D-020: structural) |
| Random / fundraise / rally / attack / never-poll / sandbag | 0 / 10.2 / 38 / 42 / 54.5 / 42% | 0 / 15.5 / 42.5 / 41 / 51 / 39% | ≤ 5, < 55, ≤ 50 ✓ |

Red after this pass: the candidate band is where pass 2 left it, at noise level (±2.2 per candidate at 2,000 matches; across five runs of this branch Okafor measured 53.5–57.7 and Venkat 53.4–59.0, so Okafor is the one genuinely over the line, by a point or two, and Alvarado and Strand are the D-037 blind spots), one matchup cell at noise level, and the two D-020 rubber-band flags. The blowout shares fell under their lines, because the debate's national shift no longer decides the tied block (D-019) all at once.

*For the designers.* (1) The symmetric-bot wash (both take the same stance 93% of the time) will be smaller between humans, who weigh "my base" and "their base" differently; the human-side check is in `docs/TESTING.md`. (2) The stance texts in `Sim/Events.luau` are placeholders in the "side with X" shape; the camps (which issues each side likes) are the balance-bearing data and are listed in each event's definition. (3) Debates at 25% of winners changed is the top of the agreed band; `DEBATE_TOPIC_WIN` 0.8 is the next notch down if human matches feel debate-heavy.


## Screens for stances and debates (v0.6.0)

### D-042 — Show what stances and debates move, on the map (no rule changes)

The designers, after the rules pass (D-039, D-040): debates deciding about one match in five is fine, but "it isn't obvious that the debates did anything unless you do a nerd breakdown of the stats"; a Choice event should make a player "calculate whether or not they should get popular in one state cuz it could decrease their popularity in a state they are relying on". No `Sim/` file, Config number, payload or View field changed.

- **One pure helper, `Shared/UI/Swing`.** It turns the sim's public arithmetic into what the screens say: `Events.stanceEffects` for a stance, `Polls.projectFlips` for leader changes (called on a stand-in `{ states = view.states }`, since `StateView` carries the `id` and `poll` it reads, in match order), `Debate.topicStates` and `Debate.moves` for a debate. The client, the F2 board and the duel harness all print its lines, so the three never disagree. `tests/ui/Swing.spec` pins the numbers, `tests/ui/Stances.spec` the screens.
- **"States you need" is public and concrete:** a state is in play when its public-poll margin between you and your best rival is within `Explain.IN_PLAY` = 5 points either way (a UI number; a 5-point lead holds about 92% on election night by `Explain.leadWinChance`, so anything inside it can still go either way). The event card lists them ("you trail WI by 2 · you lead OH by 3 · tied NV") with your projected EV. A stance's summary counts its gains among them ("4 you need: OH, PA…") and names the states you lead where it loses, closest first ("2 you lead: KY by 3…"). *Rejected:* "states the projection gives you narrowly" (the same set, said less plainly), and an EV-weighted "worth" number (the bot's closeness weight, D-041: correct but opaque to a player).
- **Stance preview on the map.** While the event card is open in a Choice week, the map shows the selected stance (default C): gains as a "+1.5" badge in your color with a soft glow, losses as a red "-1.5" badge, a state whose public-poll leader would change as FLIP with a strong glow, untouched states dimmed. Holding a chip previews that stance without selecting it; collapsing the card gives the usual map back. The flips are on today's polls from your stance alone: the opponent's stance is secret, so the preview never guesses it, and says so in the help text. Chips are full width so stance names fit on a phone; the hedge reads "(hedge, default)".
- **Debate reveal.** Each topic row adds one line, "Your Jobs win swung OH, MI, PA, GA, NC +4 more (+1.0 each) - PA flipped to you (+19 EV)", heading size and bold when a state flipped, and a red gaffe line. A flip is credited to the first topic (in order) whose states include it and that had a winner or a gaffe; flips no topic explains (the flat +0.1 or the sweep) are listed after the last topic. The map badges the net targeted points toward you (your color) or them (their color) as each topic lands, with FLIP in the new leader's color. The flat part is left out of the badges because it is the same small number everywhere and is in the summary ("Every state also got: you +0.2 · them +0.1").
- **Resolve reveal after a Choice week.** Once the stances are read out, the panel says what each side's stance gained and lost and which leaders they flipped on the polls both players saw (the server's `EventResult.flips`), the flip as a heading-size callout, and the map badges the net points per state. The week card repeats it next week.
- **Results: "What decided it".** The states debates and stances flipped on the polls, to whom and for how many EV, which of them the flipped-to side then won on election night, and the final gap against how far the flips moved the projected gap. These are poll flips, not a recount (the harness's counterfactual uses hidden true support): honest, and short.
- **Map marks.** `MapInterface.Mark` gains `badgeColor`, `halo`/`haloStrong` and `note`/`noteColor`; the halo is capped at the tap target so it stays inside the map.
- **Stopgaps replaced:** the rules pass's Choice effect line ("Take a stance: A / B / C"), the long `choiceNote`, `choiceVerb`, the Choice part of `lastEventLine`, Reveal's "points over the states that reacted" caption, and the help text, which said only the issue's states react and that a topic win adds +0.1 "everywhere else" (it is every state).

Limits: the preview adds your points straight to the shown poll shares, as `Polls.projectFlips` does; the real week also splits gains between undecided and the opponent, moves the opponent's own stance and everything else in both plans. The debate's flips are real (the polls were republished); the stance flips in the reveal are projected on the start-of-week polls, as the server reports them.

## Your base hears your stance (playtest 3)

### D-043 — A stance counts double in your base; the hedge costs there (approved reshape, Oct 22, 2026)

**Playtest 3** (v0.6.0, four Sonnet players, evaluated by a Fable agent; notes in the session scratchpad): fun 8/7/8/7 and all four would replay, debates read clearly, but in 5 of 6 Choice weeks both players took the same stance and the reveal said "flipped no state", and the hedge was called "strictly good". The cause is structural: "states you need" (D-042) is the same set for both players in a 1v1, so both read the same right answer off the same data, and two candidates who say the same thing move the same way. The hedge was never wrong because a side's camps net near zero by design (D-039) while the hedge was always positive. The designers chose, from four options, "base-weighted reactions plus a costly hedge".

**Rules.** A candidate's base is their home and strongholds (`Events.baseOf`, public once the draft is revealed). A side (A or B) counts ×`STANCE_BASE_MULT` (2) in the taker's base, both in the issue's states and in spillover. The hedge moves no state at all, except −`STANCE_HEDGE_COST` (0.5) in each base state where the issue is a top issue: those voters want you to pick a side. The platform multiplier (×1.5) still applies in the issue's states, to the hedge's cost too. Stance points are clamped to ±`STANCE_CAP` (3), not `EVENT_CAP` (2), so a doubled liked reaction on your platform reads +3 instead of being cut to 2. `STANCE_HEDGE` (0.5) is now only what a side gives in an issue state whose other top issue it neither likes nor dislikes. `Events.stanceEffect(s)` take the base as a last argument; Resolve, Bot, the event card, the rules text and the duel screens pass it.

**Why.** Drafts differ, so the same event now has a different right answer for each player, still read off public data (the state's issues and your own draft): "this stance plays well in my strongholds" is the calculation the designers asked for ("whether or not they should get popular in one state cuz it could decrease their popularity in a state they are relying on"), now with your own states carrying the stakes.

**Harness** (2,000 matches; before = D-041): stances change the winner **14.6%** (5.1%), EV swung 14.2 (4.7); RandomStance win rate **34.0%** (39.1%), so reading the card is worth more; debates 23.2% (unchanged); comeback 38.3%; candidates Okafor 52.9, Venkat 54.9, Hale 51.7, Alvarado 46.0, Strand 44.6. The bots still take the same stance **83.8%** of the time (90.7%) and never take the hedge (0%): both bots weigh the same close states, and the base is only 5–7 states of 50, so the shared battlegrounds still dominate a bot's sum. Humans, who care more about "my states", should split more; the duel playtests are the check. Variants at 1,000 matches: `STANCE_BASE_MULT` 3 moved the winner in 17.2% but pushed Okafor to 57.7 and Venkat to 62.5 (rejected); `STANCE_HEDGE_COST` 0.25 left the hedge unused (C 0%); `UNDECIDED_FLOOR` 5 with `BATTLEGROUND_MULT` 1.3 (the evaluator's proposal against one-state tug-of-wars) did not change mirroring and dropped Hale to 39.7 (not adopted; the harness does not measure "one state decided it", so that needs a metric first).

*For the designers.* The hedge is now a niche answer: right only when both sides would cost you in your own base. If playtests show nobody ever takes it, a small positive outside your base (+0.25 in the issue's other states) would bring it back without making it the safe default again.

## Decision screens and the debate focus (playtest 3)

### D-044 — The stance and the debate focus get their own screen and timer; prep becomes one focus topic (approved, Oct 23, 2026, branch `decide`)

**Asked.** The designer, after playtest 3: "On weeks with debates or event choices, they should be on a separate screen before the week decisions with a separate timer" (the UI was overwhelming). Then, while this was being built: replace the 6-point prep spread with "pick 1 focus topic", one tap, a large edge on that topic, tuned so focus matters but is not automatic; the screen must show per topic the states that care, their EV and which of them you need; a default on timeout; an equivalent for Hale's +1 prep; bots pick the topic covering the most needed EV.

**The phase.** A new input phase, `Decision` (`Sim/Decision.luau`), with `MatchState.decision` = `"Focus"` or `"Stance"`. Order inside a week:

- debate week: `Decision Focus` → `Debate` → (`Decision Stance` if the week's event is a Choice) → `Plan`
- Choice week: `Decision Stance` → `Plan`
- any other week: `Plan`

*Why this order.* The focus is chosen on its own screen right before the debate it feeds (it used to be prep in the previous week's plan, a week early and buried in the plan screen). A stance in a debate week comes after the debate, so the republished polls (D-040) are on the table when you choose; the stance resolves with the week, as before. *Rejected:* one combined screen for both (crowded, the thing the humans asked to stop), and the stance before the debate (decided on stale polls).

**Payloads and validation.** `{ focus = <one of this debate's topics> }` or `{ choice = "A"|"B"|"C" }`, validated like plans: rejected whole with a reason code (`BadFocus`, `UnexpectedFocus`, `BadChoice`, `UnexpectedChoice`, `WrongPhase`, `BadShape`), never thrown, never partially applied, normalized (unknown fields dropped). A plan that carries `focus` or `choice` is rejected (`UnexpectedFocus`, `UnexpectedChoice`); the plan payload is `{ actions }` only. Timeout default: the hedge (C) for a stance; **no focus** for the debate (see below). Both stay hidden as before: the focus in `PlayerState.debateFocus` until the debate, the stance in `PlayerState.stance` until the week resolves; `View` shows each player only their own (`view.debateFocus`, `view.stance`), `view.decision` names the step. Determinism and simultaneity are unchanged: `Decision.apply` sets every player's choice at once; specs in `tests/sim/Decision.spec`.

**The focus (replaces prep).** `topicScore = focus + platform bonus + stat + roll(0..3)`, where focus is `DEBATE_FOCUS` (**2**) on the one topic you picked and 0 on the others. An uncontested focus wins its topic 81%, ties 12%, loses 6% (Debate.odds(2)), so it usually decides the topic but not always; if both focus on the same topic the edges cancel. `DebateResult.focus` (topic index per player, 0 = none) is revealed after the debate; `DebateResult.prep[p][t]` now holds the edge each focus added, so the score breakdown reads "you 2 focus + 2 platform + 1 stat + roll 3 = 8". *Default: no focus*, not your platform's topic: it is the only default that does not pick for you, and the timeout should cost something. *Gaffe redefined:* "lost a topic you put 0 prep on" would now hit every unfocused topic (about one per debate per player) and turn the gaffe into noise, so a gaffe is now losing a topic **when you took no focus at all** (the timeout): every topic you lose then costs `DEBATE_GAFFE` (0.6) in its states. *Hale:* "Debate focus +3 instead of +2" (`focusEdge` 3; was 7 prep points of 6), the same one extra point on the topic that matters. *Rejected:* edge 1 (62% uncontested: the pick barely matters; NoFocus still lost only 33%) and 3 (94%: automatic, and Hale's +4 would be a lock).

**Bots.** `Bot.focus` picks the topic with the most expected EV a win is worth on its map (`Bot.topicValue`: each of the topic's states weighted by EV × closeness, the same "needed EV" the screen lists); ties go to the first topic. New styles `RandomFocus` and `NoFocus` for the harness; `Bot.decide` answers both Decision kinds.

**Timer.** `TIMER_DECISION` = **20 s**: one decision with the facts on one screen; the old plan timer had to cover the plan, prep and a stance in 50 s, so 20 s for a single pick is generous, and both locking in ends it at once. No time bank (it stays a plan resource). `TIMER_PLAN` unchanged. *Budget, for the designers:* D-005's worst case (every timer run out) was 1,042 s; decisions add at most 10 × 20 = 200 s (two debates and a stance in every event week with the worst deck), about 105 s with a typical deck (3.3 Choice events). That breaks the 17.5-minute worst-case line `Config.spec` pins for the old steps (still 1,042 s there; the new spec pins the decisions separately). Typical play, where both lock in within seconds, adds well under a minute. If matches run long, `TIMER_DECISION` 15 or a smaller `TIMER_PLAN` are the levers.

**Screens.** A new `Decision` screen (Router: phase `Decision` while the step is open): the Campaign header (stage "Debate focus" or "Take a stance", its own countdown, no money or AP), the map previewing the pick, one panel, and the pinned Lock in. Nothing else: no state card, plan list, week card or Bank. Focus panel (`CampaignParts/Focus`): one full-width chip per topic, "Jobs (your platform) · 15 states, 160 EV", then "You need 10 of them (103 EV): trail PA by 2 … · lead IL by 4 …", "Focus here: 5 vs their 3, plus a roll 0 to 3 each. You win 81% (19% if they focus here too).", "A win: +1.0 in each; could flip DE (+3 EV)." (all from `Shared/UI/Swing.focus`); Lock in reads "Pick a topic" until one is tapped; the map badges the picked (or held) topic's states. Stance: the event card's chips, need line and summary, as before (D-042), C preselected. The plan screen loses the prep panel and the stance chips; in a Choice week the event card says "Your stance: A · …. Secret until the week resolves."

**Harness** (2,000 matches; before = this branch's start, D-043; with `DEBATE_FOCUS` 1 / 2 / 3 for comparison, Hale fixed at 3):

| Check | Focus 2 (chosen) | Before | Focus 1 | Focus 3 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Debates change the winner | 24.1% (21.7 EV) | 23.2% (21.3 EV) | 25.3% | 22.9% |
| Hale / his topic win rate | 47.7% / 47.3% | 51.7% / 47.6% | 53.7% / 52.7% | 38.6% / 40.4% (no perk left) |
| NoFocus (timeout default) win rate | **22.8%** | — | 32.6% | 21.2% |
| RandomFocus win rate | 50.4% (49.2 ± 1.8 at 3,000) | — | 50.8% | 48.6% |
| Both bots focus on the same topic | 93.8% | — | 93.8% | 93.8% |
| Choice stances change the winner / RandomStance | 14.8% / 34.2% | 14.6% / 34.0% | — | — |
| Candidates | Okafor 54.6, Calloway 55.6, Venkat 54.2, Hale 47.7, Alvarado 47.7, Reyes 47.3, Strand 46.0, Begay 46.9 | Okafor 52.9, Calloway 56.6, Venkat 54.9, Hale 51.7, Alvarado 46.0, Reyes 49.2, Strand 44.6, Begay 44.4 | | |
| Comeback / median margin / 40+ / 100+ EV | 38.1% / 52 / 61.7% / 21.0% | 38.3% / 52 / 62.3% / 21.6% | | |

*Reading it.* The focus clearly matters: a player who lets the timer run out wins 23%. Which topic matters less between two bots: they need the same states, so they pick the same topic 94% of the time and cancel, and a random pick does as well (50%), the same structural wash D-041 found for stances before D-043. Between humans, who read "states I need" differently and can guess the opponent's pick, the choice should matter more; the "19% if they focus here too" line makes the guessing visible. *For the designers:* if playtests show both players always pick the same topic, the D-043 lever applies here too (a topic win counts double in your own base, so each player's best topic differs).

**Duel harness.** Decision steps are their own turn (`Week 4 Decision: debate focus`, `Week 5 Decision: stance`) with `focus TOPIC` and `choice a|b|c`; the README explains them. The harness default on a stance step is `choice c`, on a focus step `auto`.

## A shorter draft (playtest feedback)

### D-045 — Strongholds follow the leans; the draft is home + two nominations (approved reshape, Oct 23, 2026)

*Built on its own branch (`shortdraft`, logged there as D-044) and merged after D-044; the harness numbers below are from before D-044. With both, the old steps' worst case is 932 s (pinned in `Config.spec`) plus at most 200 s of decision screens (D-044).*

**Feedback.** "The state selection could be optimized because there's a clear correct choice for both players to make." The harness agrees: with drafted strongholds, GA, OH, MI and NC were somebody's stronghold in 98–100% of matches, and both players' first choice for home was the same state in 81% (CA or TX in 89% of homes). The designers chose a shorter draft: players pick only their home and two battleground nominations; strongholds are assigned from the map's leans.

**Rules.** When Select ends (`Match.advance` → `Draft.assignStrongholds`), each player gets their `STRONGHOLD_COUNT` (5) strongest-leaning states as strongholds: states leaning toward them, ordered by lean (largest first), then EV (larger first), then id. They own them from the map reveal on, so both players see both sets on the map (rings) and on the draft screen ("Your strongholds: … · theirs: …") before the home round. The bonuses are unchanged (+8, +4 at 20+ EV) and still applied after the last round, so a nominated stronghold still loses its bonus but keeps its lean. Then three blind rounds (`Draft.rounds` = 1 + `NOMINATION_ROUNDS`): round 0 home (a stronghold cannot be a home: it is owned), rounds 1–2 nominations (any state but a home or a battleground, either player's strongholds included). `DRAFT_PRIORITY` is A B B. `STRONGHOLD_ROUNDS` is gone; `Draft.Kind` and `DraftPick.kind` are `"Home" | "Nominate"`. The draft goes from 8 rounds and 181 s (5 s reveal + 8 × 22 s) to 3 rounds and 71 s, 110 s shorter; the worst-case match budget falls from 1,042 s to 932 s.

**Why this assignment.**
- *Fair by construction.* Leans come in EV-adjacent pairs of equal magnitude, one to each side (D-008), so in a 1v1 both players' stronghold leans are the same multiset and the EVs nearly match: the stronghold EV gap is 2 at the median, 4 at p90, 16 at worst (6,000 matches), and the side with more stronghold EV wins 48.8% (no edge). Each player's set comes only from their own leans, so the order players are served in cannot matter; a spec mirrors every lean and checks the strongholds swap seats exactly.
- *Deterministic and readable.* It is a pure function of the seeded leans: "your strongholds are the states leaning your way the most". Nothing new is random and the player can check it on the map.
- *Visible before every pick.* Assigned at the end of Select (not in `Match.new`, so the Select screen cannot read a lean's direction off `owner`, D-008).
- *Rejected.* (1) Strongholds near each player's home, after the home round: they could not be shown before the home pick, and a blind home clash would then decide strongholds too. (2) A seeded draw among each side's leaned states: less learnable and wider EV gaps. (3) Searching for the most EV-balanced subset: unnecessary at a p90 gap of 4 EV. (4) The weakest leans (spreading safe states wider): the strongest is the simpler rule and the one the designers proposed; the harness shows no reason to prefer the other.

**Why the rounds stay blind with priority.** Three rounds are short enough that the reveal-after-each-round rhythm costs little, and the home round is the only one where clashes happen: both bots' first choice for home is the same state 82% of the time, while nominations almost never clash (27% of all rounds clash ≈ 82% / 3). So priority is effectively "who gets the contested home", A by seed. Priority A wins **48.9%** of 6,000 matches (47–53% target). Merging both nominations into one round (22 s faster) was considered and not taken: it removes the reaction to the opponent's first nomination for a small saving. Priority for the home was not split or compensated because the harness shows no seat edge.

**Bots.** No change needed: `Bot.draft` already values a home and a nomination from the View (leans plus owners), and the assigned strongholds enter its margins as owned states. 0 invalid bot inputs.

**Harness** (`lune run tests/harness/balance.luau 2000`, same seeds; before = this branch's start, D-043 rules; 6,000-match run in brackets).

| Check | Before | After | Target |
|---|---|---|---|
| Draft rounds / seconds | 8 / 181 s | **3 / 71 s** | |
| Priority A win rate | 49.6% | 47.8% [48.9%] | 47–53% ✓ |
| Candidates | Okafor 52.9, **Calloway 56.6**, Venkat 54.9, Hale 51.7, Alvarado 46.0, Reyes 49.2, **Strand 44.6**, **Begay 44.4** | Okafor 54.2 [55.1], **Calloway 57.2** [54.2], Venkat 54.0 [54.3], Hale 47.5 [47.2], **Alvarado 44.6** [46.6], Reyes 50.0 [51.7], Strand 45.0 [45.1], Begay 47.7 [46.3] | 45–55% (±2.2 at 2,000) |
| Comeback rate | 38.3% | 39.9% [37.5%] | 20–40% ✓ |
| Mean / median EV margin | 63 / 52 | 60 / 50 | median ≤ 60 ✓ |
| 40+ / 100+ EV | 62.3% / 21.6% | 60.9% / 19.2% | ≤ 65% / ≤ 25% ✓ |
| Tossup EV after the draft | 24.4% | 33.4% | informational |
| Leaned states that flip | 27.2% | 20.6% | 20–40% (near the edge) |
| Stronghold EV per player / gap p50, p90, max | 95 / 14, 30, 59 | 44 / 2, 4, 16 | |
| Most common strongholds | GA 100, OH 99, MI 98, NC 98% | VA 28, MI 28, NC 28, NJ 27% | |
| Both first choices for home the same | 80.6% | 81.5% | |
| Money raised per player / GOTV spend | $37.1 / $8.3 | $29.7 / $6.7 | |
| Stances change the winner | 14.6% | 11.4% | informational |
| RandomStance win rate | 34.0% | 34.9% | ≤ 45% ✓ |

The pass/fail lines are where they were: Calloway's and Okafor's over-55 readings swap between runs at this sample size, the D-020 rubber-band flags and GOTV at 9–10% were red before. What moved: a third more of the map is still in play after the draft (strongholds no longer turn the best unleaned mid-size states, GA, OH, MI and NC, into locks; they make already-leaning states safer), so margins and blowouts fell a little. Side effects worth watching: money raised fell 19% because the old strongholds were the big fundraising pools (strongholds feed fundraisers through support share), and base-weighted stances (D-043) matter a little less because a base is now mostly already-safe states. Neither was tuned here.

*For the designers.* The stronghold choice is gone, but the home is still a clear choice for bots: CA or TX in 92% of homes, the same first choice 82% of the time, settled by priority. If humans show the same, the smallest options are (a) cap the home bonus lower in big states (e.g. +4) or drop the ×1.25 fundraiser there, so a mid-size home near your strongholds competes, or (b) let a home only be chosen among states not leaning to the opponent. Both change a mechanic's intent, so neither was done.

## Map lenses (client only)

### D-046 — One layer at a time on the map: EV, Issues, Polls, $ (no rule changes)

The designers asked for a map view switch ("EV counts, issues, polling, and fundraising amount available") and, after playtest 3, said the UI is "very crowded and overwhelming". Each node used to carry an "H B G3 *" tag, a momentum arrow, an owner ring and the fill at once. No `Sim/` file, Config number, payload or View field changed.

- **Four lenses, one row under each state's label.** A row of short pills (`Map/Lens`, drawn by `NodeMap` via the new `MapInterface.setLens`):
  - **EV** (default): the state's electoral votes, plus "B" on a battleground (every campaign effect there ×`BATTLEGROUND_MULT`).
  - **Issues**: the state's two top issues as three-letter codes (JOB, HLT, SCH, ENV, SAF, TRN, TEC, FRM); the ones on your platform are filled in your color. Issues are read from the View's issue map, so they are this match's drawn issues (D-047).
  - **Polls**: the public-poll lead in the leader's color ("+3", "tie" under half a point; "~+4" for the lean before the first poll), "i+5" for your internal poll there, the momentum ▲ in the momentum leader's color, and your ground game ("G2", placed plus planned). `Lens.pollDetails` is the hook for more per-state pills (e.g. what a poll reveals about the opponent's ground game).
  - **$**: what one more fundraiser there would raise this week, from `Explain.action` (the same number as the state card's Fundraise preview, which assumes a full pool because pool levels are hidden). A state your drafted plan already empties (under $0.05) is dimmed.
- **What stays in every lens:** the fill by public-poll leader, the owner ring, the selection ring, the route, and the Tags layer when it is relevant: the event's * on the states it singles out, stance previews and reveal swings (D-042), and the Draft's H / B / 1 / 2 picks.
- **What moved off the default map:** "H" (the owner ring and the state card say it), "B" (into the EV row), "G" units and the momentum arrows (into Polls). The Campaign's caption shows the current lens's one-line legend.
- **The switch** (`Map/LensToggle`): four 54×44 chips, EV / Issues / Polls / $, beside the caption on the Campaign screen and over the map on the Draft screen (inside the map's 0.34 share on a phone, so the panel keeps its height). The choice is a module-level value, so it holds for the whole session across screens. Election night keeps the plain map. *Merged after D-044 and D-045 (built on its own branch, logged there as D-044):* the Decision screen (focus and stance) keeps its own map with only the pick's preview and no lens chips, so a single decision stays uncluttered; stance previews therefore no longer appear on the Campaign map.

*Rejected:* a lens that recolors the fill (e.g. issues as a heat map): the fill is the one thing the designers said must always mean "who leads". Rich text to color parts of one label: the fake engine and the audit do not measure it; separate pills do the same job and stay testable.

## Drawn issues and colors, unique candidates (playtest feedback, Oct 22, 2026)

### D-047 — Each match draws its top issues; niche issues hit harder; colors are drawn (approved reshape)

*Built on its own branch (`randissues`, logged there as D-044 and D-045) and merged after D-044 to D-046; the harness numbers below are from before those. The debate focus (D-044) and the Issues lens (D-046) read the match's issue map from the View.*

**Feedback.** The designers: "Character select color should be randomly assigned, no need to pick." and "Issues and their associated EV values should be random. It makes no sense to pick any choice but the top couple issues." Then: "niche issues hit harder": a big issue should give a small boost across many states and a niche one a big boost in few, so each platform pick is worth about the same and the right pick depends on where you plan to fight.

**Colors.** Gone from the Select payload, its validation, the client, the bots and the debug commands (an old client's or script's `color` is ignored). `Match.new` shuffles the six colors with the seed (`Rng.fork(rng, "colors")`) and deals them by seat: distinct, deterministic, no seat favored (each seat gets each color 1 time in 6; `Match.spec`). The color is public from Select on (View), shown as a line on both Select steps and everywhere it was before. `pickedColor` and the playtest-1 clash notice are removed.

**Issue map.** `Sim/IssueMap.draw` gives each state two different top issues per match, from `Rng.fork(rng, "issues")`, so other seeded streams (leans, topics, deck) are unchanged. A state's old fixed issues are now its `flavor` (`StateData`), drawn `ISSUE_FLAVOR_WEIGHT` (4) times as often as the others: about 39% of drawn slots match the flavor against 25% for a blind draw, so Farms still leans to the Plains. A seeded local search (replace one state's issue, or trade issues between two states; keep any move that is no worse) then pulls every issue's total EV within `ISSUE_EV_BAND` (±20%) of the 134.5 mean, 108–161, and every issue into `ISSUE_MIN_STATES`..`ISSUE_MAX_STATES` (9–17) states; it stops as soon as the map is in band (about 1.6 ms a match; `IssueMap.spec` checks 300 seeds are all in band). The map (`MatchState.issueMap`) is public in every View from Select on. Everything that read `StateData.issues` reads the match's map: Resolve, Events (stance effects take the map), Debate (`topicStates`/`moves` take it), Draft auto-fill, the bot, Explain, PlanDraft, Swing, the state card, the debug board and the duel harness (`map.txt` is per match now). Tried first: a ±10% band (spread 22–25 EV): flat, so with the niche multiplier the platform choice would carry almost no information; ±20% keeps a real spread (median 45 EV, at most 53, against 54 on the old fixed map) for the multiplier to trade against.

**Niche issues hit harder.** `IssueMap.platformMults`: a rally or ad on a platform issue in a state where it is a top issue gets `ISSUE_MULT_STATE` + (`ISSUE_MULT_BOTH` − `ISSUE_MULT_STATE`) × (134.5 ÷ the issue's EV)^`ISSUE_NICHE_POWER`, capped at `ISSUE_NICHE_MAX` (2.0), rounded to hundredths: ×1.5 at the mean, ×1.72 for a 108-EV issue, ×1.39 for a 161-EV one. `Resolve.issueMult` takes the match's table (`MatchState.platformMults`, public in the View). The Select chips show both numbers ("Farms / 112 EV · ×1.67"); the help text gives this match's range and your own platform's.

*Tuning.* Two harness styles run the standard bot on the match's three biggest (`TopIssues`) or three smallest (`NicheIssues`) issues against the standard bot. Power 1 (EV × bonus equal for every issue, the obvious formula) left niche platforms behind: Top 50.4%, Niche 44.0% (1,500 games each). Big issues are worth more than their rally bonus says, because debates move a topic's states and Choice stances spill through top issues, both by EV. Power 2: 48.9 / 46.5; 2.5: 48.7 / 47.6; 3: 48.8 / 47.6 (2,500 each, ±1.0); the final harness run measured 53.0 / 47.4 at 800 each (±1.8). 2.5 is the number: the gap is within noise and the multiplier's spread (×1.39–×1.72) is big enough to see on the chip. A ±15% band was tried too (Top 49.6, Niche 44.1 at power 1): narrowing the spread does not fix the lean, the multiplier does. Mixed platforms win slightly more than either extreme. The bot now picks its platform at random (it used to take the most-EV issues with noise), since no issue is the right pick before the draft.

**Harness** (`lune run tests/harness/balance.luau 2000`; before = this branch's start, measured the same way).

| Check | After | Before |
|---|---|---|
| Issue EV per match (min / median / max, every issue) | 108 / 130–137 / 161 | fixed: Schools 106 … Jobs 160 |
| Spread in a match (top − bottom) | median 45, max 53 | 54, every match |
| Bot platform = the 3 highest-EV issues | **2.2%** | **29.4%** (400 matches) |
| Platform picks per issue | 12.3–12.7% each | Jobs 30.7, Safety 27.4, Technology 16.0, Health 14.8, Transit 6.2, Environment 4.8, Farms 0.2, Schools 0.0% |
| TopIssues / NicheIssues vs the standard bot | 53.0 / 47.4% | — |
| Candidates | Okafor **58.3**, Calloway 53.5, Venkat 54.5, Hale 46.5, Alvarado 45.8, Reyes 48.1, **Strand 44.0**, Begay 49.6 | Okafor 52.9, **Calloway 56.6**, Venkat 54.9, Hale 51.7, Alvarado 46.0, Reyes 49.2, **Strand 44.6**, **Begay 44.4** |
| Priority A / comeback | 51.1 / 39.5% | 49.6 / 38.3% |
| Median margin / 40+ / 100+ EV | 50 / 59.5 / 18.9% | 52 / 62.3 / 21.6% |
| GOTV / debates / stances change the winner | 10.9 / 22.4 / 16.2% | 9.3 / 23.2 / 14.6% |
| RandomStance / NeverPoll win rate | 29.1 / **57.2%** | 34.0 / 54.5% |

Red after this pass: Okafor over the line and Strand under it (both have swung ±3 between runs since D-041; ±2.2 is one standard deviation at 2,000 matches), the Okafor–Alvarado cell, the D-020 underdog flag, and NeverPoll at 57% (400 games, ±2.5; it was 54.5 before). Candidates now play on a different map every match, so per-candidate home-region advantages from the fixed map are gone; a candidate pass with 4,000+ matches is the next step if Okafor stays high.

### D-048 — Two players never share a candidate; Select in two steps (approved reshape)

**Feedback.** "Two players must never have the same candidate", and Select becomes step 1 candidate, step 2 issues (color automatic).

**Rule.** A candidate is claimed by whoever claims it first (`Match.claim`, pure; claims are public in the View as `claims`, so the other player sees it as taken at once). Claims that arrive in the same server step are settled together in a seeded order (`Rng.fork(match.rng, "claims:<n>")`): a coin flip, never seat 1; the loser gets the `CandidateTaken` notice and picks again. A claim for a held candidate, or a lock-in of it, is rejected with `CandidateTaken` and never applied; one claim per player (`AlreadyClaimed`). Locking in a selection claims its candidate if it is still free, so bots, the debug `select` command and old clients work unchanged. On timeout the default is your claim, else a free candidate from the seed; `resolveSelect` settles any remaining clash (two unclaimed defaults) in a seeded order. Practice bots claim their candidate as Select opens, and a bot whose candidate is taken takes a free one. The balance harness already excluded mirror matchups; it now records the candidates as played.

**Client.** Select step 1: the eight cards (the opponent's shows "Taken by your opponent", greyed and inert) and the color line; "Next: issues" sends `Claim` (`Remotes`: `{ matchId, step, input = candidate }`). Step 2 opens when the server confirms the claim: the color line, "2. Issues for <name>", the hint, and eight two-line chips with this match's EV and multiplier. Both steps fit a phone and an iPad without scrolling (UI audit 0). Debug: `claim CANDIDATE`; `select CANDIDATE ISSUE ISSUE ISSUE` (a color word is skipped).

**Timer.** `TIMER_SELECT` 30 → 38: two steps need more than one, and 38 is the most the 17.5-minute worst case allows (D-005: the worst case was exactly 1,050 s on this branch; with D-045's shorter draft the old steps' worst case is 940 s, plus at most 200 s of decision screens, D-044).

## Polls reveal ground game (playtest feedback)

### D-049 — An internal poll shows their ground game; a unit costs $2.5 (approved reshape + tuning, Oct 23, 2026)

*Built on its own branch (`polls`, logged there as D-044) and merged after D-044 to D-048; the harness numbers below are from before those. On playtest4 the "opp G3" tag lives in the map's Polls lens (`Lens.pollDetails`, D-046), not on the default map.*

**Feedback.** "Polling doesn't have any obvious use cases" and "Is ground game overpowered?" Both were fair. A unit was the cheapest point of margin in the game ($2 for +1.5, $1.33 a point, never saturating, up to 4 a state) against $1.9 a point for an ad that saturates; it was invisible to the opponent until election night; and paid polls only sharpened a support number nothing else used. The designers chose "polls reveal ground game, plus trim ground game a bit".

**Rules.** An internal poll (`Poll` or `RegionPoll`) now also reports every player's ground-game units in each polled state, exact, as of the end of the week polled (`InternalPoll.gotv`). The poller keeps the latest reading per state (`PlayerState.scouted`, a `Scout` of state, week and units) after the poll itself expires, so a week-8 reading still shows in week 10, marked as possibly stale. Only `View.build` for the poller carries it (`View.scouted`); `tests/sim/View.spec` checks that another seat's readings never reach the view and that units placed after a reading never change it. No public hint ("none / some / heavy") was added: a free public signal would do the poll's job for nothing.

**Tuning.** `GOTV_COST` 2 → **2.5** ($1.67 a point; Walt Begay still pays $1, so his perk is worth a little more). Chosen over `GOTV_PER_UNIT` 1.5 → 1.2 because a unit keeps its visible +1.5, and because at 1,500 matches `GOTV_PER_UNIT` 1.2 and 1.3 dropped "GOTV changes the winner" to 6–9% even with a keener bot (1.4 held 10% but is a smaller trim, $1.43 a point), while $2.5 with the bot changes below keeps it at 10%. The Config spec's D-021 check (a unit beats a mismatched ad per dollar, loses to a matched one) still holds.

**Bot.** From week 7 (`scoutFromWeek`) the poll goes to the closest state by EV × closeness (not just 8+ EV states), or a whole region when its close states are worth 1.5× the singles its price buys; no paid poll while the opponent's cash shows Low; free polls first. Every unit a reading saw counts against the bot's margin there (`Estimate.theirGotv`). A fresh internal poll now keeps the public poll's error variance in the bot's sigma (`freshPollVar` 1): with the exact ±1 sigma the bot piled its money into one razor-close state, and perfect free knowledge of true support made it *lose* 65% of mirrored matches. Ground game starts a week earlier (`gotvFromWeek` 6) and is valued at `gotvPremium` 1.5 (was 1.3) so the dearer unit is still bought.

**Harness** (`lune run tests/harness/balance.luau`, 2,000 matches; "before" is this branch's start on the same seeds; `tests/harness/scout.luau` is new: 2,000 NeverPoll-vs-Balanced matches with spend).

| Check | After | Before | Target |
|---|---|---|---|
| Candidates | Okafor 53.3, Calloway 54.5, **Venkat 55.3**, Hale 47.3, Alvarado 47.6, Reyes 47.7, Strand 45.8, Begay 48.7 | Okafor 52.9, **Calloway 56.6**, Venkat 54.9, Hale 51.7, Alvarado 46.0, Reyes 49.2, **Strand 44.6**, **Begay 44.4** | 45–55% |
| GOTV changes the winner / average spend | **10.4%** / $7.7 | 9.3% / $8.3 | ≥ 10% |
| NeverPoll win rate (balance side check, 400 games / scout harness, 2,000) | 55.5% / **55.7%** | 54.5% / 58.1% | designers: well under 50% |
| Polls / ground game spend per player (scout harness, Balanced) | $5.7 / $7.5 | — | — |
| Debates change the winner / comeback / 100+ EV | 24.0% / 38.7% / 19.0% | 23.2% / 38.3% / 21.6% | ✓ |

**Not met: NeverPoll stays about 55%.** The bots cannot use what a poll shows, and the reason is structural, not a bug. Measured with an oracle that hands the Balanced bot information for free (1,500 matches; the no-poll mirror measured 52.4% on these seeds): knowing the opponent's exact ground game in *every* state from week 7 left NeverPoll at 53.3%, worth nothing, because two bots that weigh the same close states put their units in the same states, so the reading only confirms what each bot would have done anyway; knowing true support in every state was worth 10 points (41.9%), but realistic polling (the five closest states a week, nearly free) was worth only 3–5, and at $1 a state a poll costs the bot more than it returns (each $1 is worth about 0.8 points of win rate in a mirrored match). Every paid-poll schedule tried (1–4 a week, regions at $1–3, from week 4, 7, 9 or 10, carrying old readings forward with `POLL_RHO`) measured 53–63%. Humans differ in the way that matters: they do not mirror, so "their 3 units in a state I thought was safe" changes a human's plan where it does not change a bot's. The TESTING checklist asks the playtesters whether a reading changed a plan.

*For the designers.* If the playtests also find polls unused, the levers are, in order: a cheaper single poll ($0.5; the bot then loses only 1–3 points to NeverPoll), a cheaper region poll ($1.5–2), or making the reading public-facing in a limited way (the poll also reveals how many states they have ground game in, not where). Strand's two free polls are now worth a little more to a human (they scout), and about the same to the bot.

## UI style v2 (client only)

### D-050 — Style v2 foundation: a sheet, coach tips, a "now" line, and audit rules for clutter (no rule changes, Oct 23, 2026)

**Feedback.** "A deep UI beautification overhaul. Make it more intuitive, less cluttered, and easier to understand and pick up", after "The UI is very crowded and overwhelming." Players: Mac and iPad; the audience on Roblox skews young.

**What we saw.** A headless renderer (`tests/lib/Snapshot.luau`, driven by `tests/ui/snap.luau`) now draws every screen at 390x844, 1024x768 and 1280x720. The layout fixes of D-030 hold (0 audit problems), but the screens are crowded in other ways: Campaign's actions are 1,100+ px below the fold, instructions are 13 px status lines, labels are jargon (EV, AP, ×1.41, i+5, G2), and several screens carry 30–60-word paragraphs. The full audit is `docs/UI-STYLE.md` §1.

**Decisions.** (1) One primary action per screen (unchanged) plus a "what to do now" line under the header. (2) Progressive disclosure: on screen a label, a pill or a number; a sentence behind "Why?"; the full breakdown in a sheet. (3) The map is the hero on Campaign; the state card opens on a tap and starts with the actions. (4) First-match coach tips, once per session (a saved "seen" needs the profile, a server change, so not now). (5) Warning text gets its own orange so "careful" never reads as "press here"; the accent stays the primary's color. (6) Four new audit rules make clutter measurable: palette colors only (`tokenColor`), at most 4 text sizes on a screen (`textSizes`), button labels at most 8 words (`labelWords`), no text over 30 words outside a details area (`wallOfText`). The screens that break them today are listed in `Audit.PENDING` and `tests/ui/Style.spec.luau` ratchets the list down. 30 words: the shortest limit that leaves every one-line hint and the Lobby untouched while catching the state card note, the draft rules, the focus details and the debate swing lines.

**Work split.** Three packages rebuild the screens in parallel on this foundation (`docs/UI-STYLE.md` §5): WP-A Lobby, Select, Draft; WP-B Decision, Campaign and the map; WP-C reveals, debate, Election, Results, Help and the coach-tip copy. No gameplay number changed.

### D-051 — The primary color is pink, not yellow (no rule changes, Oct 23, 2026)

**Problem.** The accent (the one primary button per screen, the selection outline, the timer and its bar, the coach-tip outline, the selected map ring) was a light yellow (255, 214, 102). The Amber player color (232, 164, 40) sits next to it (CIEDE2000 ΔE 13), so Amber's name, votes and map fill read as "press here". The designers chose to keep Amber as a player color (UI-STYLE §5, question 3).

**Decision.** The accent is **pink (255, 128, 214)**. It is the widest gap on the color wheel left by the six player colors (Crimson 352°, Amber 38°, Emerald 154°, Teal 184°, Cobalt 220°, Violet 269°) and the status colors (bad 0°, warn 26°, good 140°): its hue is 319°, at least 33° from each, and its smallest ΔE to any of them is 25 (Violet), against 13 before. Cyan, the other suggestion, sits 17 ΔE from Teal; white on blue would put the primary next to Cobalt and the body text. Dark text (`onAccent`) on it is 8.3:1. Every use goes through the `accent` token, so nothing else changed; `tests/ui/Helpers.spec.luau` now checks the hue gap (≥ 30°) and the label contrast (≥ 4.5:1). The debug panel keeps its own colors.

### D-052 — Style v2 merged and polished; the audit's pending list is empty (no rule changes, Oct 23, 2026)

**Merge.** WP-A (Lobby, Select, draft), WP-C (reveals, debate, election, results, help, coach tips) and WP-B (decision, campaign, map) merged into `ui-overhaul` in that order. The overlaps were the shared specs (Stances, Lobby, Campaign), the snap shot list, `Audit.PENDING`, the fake engine's `Vector2.new()` default and the coach-tip default (both packages fixed the same crash; both fixes kept), and the changelog. Where WP-B's map and WP-C's reveal both changed how the reveal badges states, WP-C's rule wins (flips and the 3 biggest moves; `Words.topMarks`) and WP-B's renders it (every moved state glows). `Audit.PENDING` is empty: all 96 shots (32 states × 3 sizes) report 0 audit findings.

**Polish** (from reading every snap at 390x844, 1024x768 and 1280x720): on the reveal map a flip says just FLIP (the "+1.0 FLIP" tags covered neighbours on the debate map); the reveal's now line keeps the action and state only (it wrapped on a phone); events say "20% more" instead of "×1.2", action costs "Flight $2.0" instead of "fly $2.0"; Results' breakdown and Help's exact rules say votes and actions (`Words.inVotes`, `Words.plain`, display-only: `Swing` and `Explain` keep their output); the debate's flat-points line drops a side with nothing; Results cards split votes and states; the map reveal on wide screens hides the still-empty draft board; small spacing fixes on the private-match screen and the empty plan. Version 0.9.0, phase "UI overhaul".

**Left for later.** The election spotlight repeats the newest feed row (deliberate: the spotlight is the moment, the feed the history). The Northeast column on the draft map still crowds its EV numbers at 1280x720. The map reveal's Strongholds card sits on a raised fill inside the panel (WP-A's choice, a card one step up rather than a card in a card).

## Polish pass (client only)

### D-053 — Motion and sound foundation; presentation timings live in Motion and Audio, not Sim/Config (no rule changes, Oct 23, 2026)

**Ask.** "Needs to look polished. Add animations and basic sound effects too." The plan (polish GAMEPLAN, §6) builds one foundation first, then three packages in parallel on it: board and map (WP-1), reveals, election and results (WP-2), setup, help and icons (WP-3).

**Where the numbers live.** CLAUDE.md says every gameplay number lives in `Sim/Config.luau`. Animation durations, easings, volumes, cooldowns and pitches are not gameplay: they change no rule, no outcome and nothing the server checks, and the server never sees them. They live in `src/client/UI/Motion.luau` (`Motion.t`, `Motion.ease` and the effect constants) and `src/client/UI/Audio.luau` (`Audio.CUES`), each with a comment, so `Sim/` stays pure and a designer tuning feel never touches a rules file. `tests/ui/Tokens.spec.luau` keeps them out of Config and keeps `TweenService`, `Sound`s and `Enum.Font` literals out of every other client file, so the reduced-motion setting, the mute button and a font swap reach everything. Display-only thresholds (the map bands, `Colors.BANDS`: toss-up < 3, lean < 8, likely < 15 points) follow the same rule. Reveal and election pacing stays where it is: driven by the server's deadlines (`Config.TIMER_*`), which are gameplay.

**Decisions.** (1) One `Motion` module with fixed timings (press 0.07 s … hero 0.45 s) that never blocks input or waits on `Tween.Completed` while planning. (2) Roblox's Reduce Motion (`GuiService.ReducedMotionEnabled`) and a session toggle turn decorative motion off and informational motion instant; enter/exit keep a 0.1 s fade. (3) Thirteen cues with per-cue cooldowns, a 4-voice cap, ± pitch jitter, fallbacks derived from working cues by pitch, and a `maxLength` cut: the lead found Cute_Pop (2.7 s) and Indicator (3.4 s) long enough to smear the reveal's beats and election calls, so planAdd stops at 0.6 s and lockIn/call at 0.8 s. All eleven ids load in the published place; auditioning by ear is in `docs/TESTING.md`. (4) Mute and reduced motion are session-only (a saved setting needs the profile, a server change). (5) Every Kit button presses and clicks; the timer is warn under 10 s and danger under 5 s with a tick; lock-in flashes. (6) Builder Sans + Fredoka One through `Kit.font` with fallbacks; caption 13 → 14; top-light gradients and hairlines instead of shadows; a root `UIScale` (1 to 1.35) for big screens only, so every snapshot size is unchanged. (7) Icons: twelve Lucide icons with glyph fallbacks until the images are uploaded.

**Deviations from the plan, for the packages.** `Motion.stagger` calls `fn(item, index, delay)` for every item at once and the effect takes the delay (`Motion.enter(inst, dy?, delay?)`, `Motion.pop(inst, from?, delay?)`): items start hidden instead of flashing visible until their turn. A chip plays `select` when tapped (not from `setSelected`, which screens also call while refreshing). The Router fades the new screen in but drops the old one at once (two live screens would share element names). The header's sound button steps aside when the title would not fit beside it (on a phone: stance and debate-prep weeks, election night); `TopBar` now reads `header.rightWidth` at each layout. `ActionBar` flashes by itself when an enabled primary becomes "Locked in…"; the label stays "Locked in" (the specs and screens read it).

### D-054 — Polish merged; icons uploaded; plan badges on the rim (no rule changes, Oct 23, 2026)

**Merge.** WP-1 (board and map), WP-2 (reveals, election night, results) and WP-3 (setup, help, settings, icons) merged into `polish` in that order, each followed by the full checks. Code merged cleanly: the one shared source file, `Screens/Campaign.luau`, took WP-1's motion and WP-2's `map = map` hook for the reveal side by side (WP-1's plan badges only touch the map while planning; WP-2's reveal rings and pulses during reveals). The changelog and Studio checklist kept every package's section. One overlap needed a check: a plan the server refuses now reaches both WP-3's toast (shake + `error`) and WP-1's action bar (shake + `error`) in the same frame. The `error` cue's 0.3 s cooldown keeps it to a single play; `CampaignParts.spec` now asserts the rejection toast is up and exactly one `error` played. The 4-voice cap drops a cue silently; every cue count the specs assert (one `planAdd` per add, one `error` per refusal, `victory` or `defeat` once per match) holds with the cap in place.

**Icons.** The twelve Lucide PNGs are uploaded; their ids are in `UI/Icons.luau` and `assets/icons/README.md`, glyphs kept as the fallback for an empty id. The headless specs now draw images, so the glyph-path specs blank an id to test it, and the header spec expects the `volume-x` image (no slash) when the icon is an image. Snapshots draw a `Kit.icon` image as its glyph in its tint inside a dashed box, so the shots still show which icon sits where.

**Tuned from the snapshots** (display only). The planned-action badge sat inside a small circle's top-right corner and hid half of the state's letters on a 390 px phone ("O▸" for OH); it is now centered at (0.95, 0.05) of the node, on the ring. It may overhang its node: new audit attribute `Overhang = true` skips only the "outside its parent" half of the `contained` rule, never the screen edge (`Audit.spec` covers both). The Results fallback reason said "Dana carried 20 states to 31" when the winner had fewer states; it now reads "Dana won the bigger states (20 to 31)". The `23a-results-loss` shot now shows the loser's side (it showed the winner's totals under "You lost").

**Result.** 948 specs pass; 111 shots (37 × 3 sizes), 0 audit findings; `Audit.PENDING` empty. Version 0.10.0, phase "Polish: motion and sound". No Config number changed.

## Colors and the Color Pass

### D-055 — Red and blue by default; pick your color with a Game Pass (approved, Oct 23, 2026)

*Its tie rule (the loser of a same-color tie is dealt a default for the match) is superseded by D-061: colors are claimed in Select, first come, first served.*

**Ask.** The designers: "make red and blue the default assigned options with an option to choose your color for robux". This replaces D-047's "colors are drawn" (a seeded shuffle of all six).

**Rule (Sim, pure).** `Match.new(config, seed, playerIds, colorPicks?)`; `Match.dealColors` does the work. `colorPicks` is keyed by player id and holds only Color Pass owners' saved colors; a name that is not one of the six is ignored. A picker gets their pick; two players who picked the same color are settled by a seeded coin flip (`Rng.fork(rng, "colorPicks")`, in the fixed color order) and the loser is dealt like a non-picker. Non-pickers are dealt, in a seeded seat order, Crimson and Cobalt first (their order a seeded coin flip), then Emerald, Amber, Violet and Teal in a seeded order (`Rng.fork(rng, "colors")`), skipping taken colors. So two players without picks are always one Crimson and one Cobalt, each seat gets Crimson half the time (D-001), a picker who takes Crimson leaves the other Cobalt, and a picker who takes Emerald leaves the other red or blue. No other seeded stream changed: `lune run tests/harness/balance.luau 400` prints the same report before and after (only the run time differs). `Match.spec` covers all of it.

**Robux.** A Game Pass, "Pick Your Color", permanent. Its id is `Net/Products.COLOR_PASS_ID` (not `Sim/Config`: it is not a gameplay number). While it is 0 nothing is checked, the lobby status carries no `colorPass` and the client shows no color option: every match is red and blue. The server never takes the client's word for ownership: `server/PassService` asks `MarketplaceService:UserOwnsGamePassAsync` when a player joins (pcall'd, three tries 2 s and 4 s apart, cached for the visit), marks a buyer as an owner on the server's `PromptGamePassPurchaseFinished` with `wasPurchased`, and asks again when a player it does not know as an owner tries to save a color (a failed check at join, a purchase made on the website). A failed check is "does not own": that player gets red or blue, never an error. At match start the server passes `colorPicks` only for owners with a saved color.

**Profile.** `Profile.color` (optional, one of the six; nil = auto). `migrate` keeps a valid one and drops anything else; old profiles load as auto. No `Profile.VERSION` bump: the field is optional, and a bump would make servers still on 0.10 treat these profiles as "from the future" and stop saving those players' results. A 0.10 server that saves such a profile drops the color (the player picks again). New op `SetColor { color? }`: a named color needs the pass (`NoColorPass`), an unknown name is `UnknownColor`, unreadable data is `NotSaved`; no color = back to auto, always allowed.

**Client.** The Lobby gets a full-width "Color: Teal" / "Color: red or blue" button under the settings chips, only when the status carries `colorPass`. It opens a sheet ("Your color") with six swatches in rows of three and "Auto: red or blue". Without the pass the swatches are greyed (labels muted, dots faded), Auto is outlined, one line says "Pick your color with the Color Pass." and the one secondary is "Get the Color Pass", which calls `MarketplaceService:PromptGamePassPurchase(LocalPlayer, id)` from the client (the docs' own pattern); the server hears the outcome and sends a new status. With the pass a tap saves the color and the outline follows the server's answer. Select's line reads "Your color: Crimson"; the old "drawn at random" fallback now says "red or blue".

**Colors checked, not changed.** Crimson (206, 52, 72) is a clear red (hue 352°) and Cobalt (52, 104, 214) a clear blue (221°), 41 ΔE apart. Against the pink accent: Crimson 30 ΔE and 33° of hue, Cobalt 37 ΔE (D-051's floor is Violet at 25 and a 30° hue gap; `Helpers.spec` still passes).

**Left for later.** Crimson sits 9.7 ΔE from the `bad` red (232, 84, 84). That was true before, but a player was Crimson 1 match in 6 and is now Crimson in half of them: on the stance preview a Crimson player's gains (their color) and losses (`bad`) are both red, told apart by the + and − on the badges. If playtests find that confusing, the cheapest fix is a deeper Crimson or a different loss color in the preview only. The pass id is 0 until the designers create the pass (`docs/TESTING.md`, "Setting up the Color Pass").

**Tests.** `tests/ui/ColorPass.spec` runs the real server and client on the fake engine (FakeRoblox now has a MarketplaceService: `world.market`, `setColorPass`, `setOwnsPass`, `finishPassPurchase`): an owner's saved color reaches the match, a non-owner's is ignored and their `SetColor` refused, a failed check falls back and one retry recovers, invalid colors are rejected, a pass id of 0 hides everything, and buying from the sheet unlocks it. Shots `01b-lobby-color`, `02c-lobby-color-locked` and `02d-lobby-color-owned` report 0 audit findings at all three sizes (120 shots in all, every one at 0). Version 0.11.0. No Config number changed.

## Front-page polish (client only)

### D-056 — Losses are orange; a navy stage, chunky buttons and the parts for the big moments (no rule changes, Oct 23, 2026)

**Ask.** The designers: "make the loss color orange and then seriously polish the appearance overall. it should look like it could appear on the front page and fit right in." The art direction splits the work into a foundation (this entry) and three packages built on it (the map, the setup and campaign panels, the moments); this entry is the foundation.

**Orange means against you; red never means bad.** Red and blue are the default player colors (D-055), and the old `bad` red sat 9.7 ΔE from Crimson. New token `loss` = 255, 122, 40 (hue 23°, 45 ΔE and 31° of hue from Crimson, 7.2:1 for dark text on it), with `lossEdge` and `onLoss`. `warn`, `danger` and `bad` are now the same color as `loss`: one orange for "you lost this" and "careful", because two oranges told apart by value alone would not survive a kid's glance. Every caller keeps working through the old names. A battleground is good news, so it leaves orange: a white tag (the map's numbers, the Draft round pill, the state card's pill); "Taken" on Select is muted information, not a loss; Help's "Ground game" row is muted. Amber moves to 240, 186, 36 (hue 44°: gold) so it stays 42 ΔE from the loss orange. New audit rule `lossText`: an orange tag or number of one or two words must carry a sign (−, ▼, "lost") or a `Warn` attribute (status warnings, the timer, a destructive button), so orange never carries meaning alone. Debate verdicts became filled pills (green "You win", orange "Rex wins", plain "Tie") to pass it.

**Navy stage.** Every neutral moves from grey-black to navy (background 13, 17, 30; surface 24, 30, 48; raised 38, 46, 70; line 70, 80, 112; text 240, 243, 250; muted 158, 168, 194), each step about 7 L* apart, so the card gradients finally show. `Colors.textOn` (a player color a quarter of the way to `text`) and `Kit.playerText` give readable player-colored text (Crimson text 4.7:1 on surface); the audit's palette includes the six variants.

**Chunky, lit parts.** Primary and secondary buttons get a lip (4 px `accentLip`, 3 px `raisedLip`) the face presses down onto; that needs a holder frame around the face (`Kit.outer`), which takes the caller's placement and follows later Size, Visible and LayoutOrder changes. The primary's label is Fredoka One at 20. Every card, button, sheet and the action bar has a border lit from the top (`Kit.bevel`: the stroke carries its own gradient, because a highlight frame would be moved by a list layout). Shadows (`Kit.shadow`) and glows (`Kit.glow`) are images that sit beside or under their object; in a list or grid there is no room for a sibling, so list items get their depth from the bevel. Sheets are radius 16 with a shadow and scale in on wide screens; the action bar sits on a lit plate with a shadow cast upward; the header's time bar is 4 px, rounded, with a glowing dot. New parts for the packages: `Kit.stamp`, `Kit.monogram`, `Kit.raceBar`, `Kit.wordmark`, `Kit.eyebrow`, `Kit.iconPlate`, filled and white pills (`Kit.pill`, `Kit.paintPill`), and `Motion.stamp`, `shine`, `breathe`, `pulseOnce` (all decorative: nothing under reduced motion). The color sheet's swatches are monograms of the player's initial.

**Assets.** Two images made by `assets/ui/make.py` (PIL): `ui-shadow.png` (9-slice, 96x96) and `ui-glow.png` (radial, 256x256), plus an unused `ui-dots.png`. Their ids go in `src/client/UI/Assets.luau`; until then the helpers build their image labels with an empty id and the game shows no shadows or glows (nothing errors).

**Renderer.** The headless snapshots (`tests/lib/Snapshot.luau`) now draw UIGradient (fills, text, strokes), the shadow and glow stand-ins, UIScale, CanvasGroup transparency and per-corner radii, so reviewers see what Studio shows. The fake engine gained `GetPropertyChangedSignal` on instances, `Rect` and multi-keypoint sequences.

**Departures from the art direction.** Lips are darker than specified (`accentLip` 158, 58, 126 and `raisedLip` 9, 12, 22): at 196, 84, 160 and 22, 27, 44 the lips vanished against the shaded face and the surface plate. A selected chip gets no glow (it would have to be a sibling inside a list). Coach tips keep md padding (a card's 14 would push Select's first step below the fold on a phone). Color-sheet monograms are 36 px, not 40, so color names keep body size on a phone. No version bump; the lead writes the merge entry.

### D-058 — White accent replaces pink (no rule changes, Oct 23, 2026)

**Ask.** The designers: the pink accent (D-051) is distracting; make it white. Their reason: pink pulled the eye on every screen for no meaning, and white keeps color for meaning only (players red and blue, green gains, orange losses).

**Tokens.** `accent` 244, 246, 252 (a near-white a touch brighter than `text`; channel spread 8, so it has no hue of its own); `accentEdge` 196, 204, 224 (cool light grey, the primary's 2 px edge); `accentLip` 110, 122, 158 (mid slate: 2.4:1 against the face's shaded bottom, 3.9:1 on the action-bar plate, 4.4:1 on the stage, so the lip reads under a white face on navy); `onAccent` stays `background` (17.4:1). The primary is a solid white face with a navy Fredoka label on a slate lip. Selection stays a 2 px accent outline plus `raisedHover`: by eye at all three sizes the white outline reads clearly next to white labels, and the map's selected ring stays 3 px.

**What changed meaning.** "White = special" (D-056) collided with "white = press here / selected", so the battleground mark is no longer a white fill. It is an outlined tag: `field` fill, a 1.5 px `text` outline (`Kit.MARK_STROKE`, thinner than the selection's 2 px) and white bold text after a ★ (`Kit.STAR`): `Kit.pill(text, "white")` / `Kit.paintPill` (which adds the star to the pill's text and takes it off for any other tone), the map's vote tag (`LensPart.outlined`, "★13"), the state card and the Draft round pill (now only on a battleground round; the home round is a plain tag). The map key says "★ and a white outline: a battleground". Text that used the accent only for emphasis is `text` now (the reveal intro, Results' "next time" line, the state card's action icons, election night's "reaches 270!"); the coach tip's outline and a notice toast's stroke are `muted` (information, not selection), and "Got it" is a lipless secondary button so it still reads as a button. Where white flashed to white it now flashes to something visible: the lock-in flash dips the face to `accentEdge`, election night's 270 marker blinks `line` and back. The wordmark keeps its white face with a slate (`accentLip`) outline, a `line` drop and a softer white glow (0.78); the race bar's crossing flash is a white glow pulse. The timer and its bar are white, orange under the same thresholds as before.
